People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m having trouble keeping my mind on one thing. At work in the warehouse, any little thing—a beeper, a forklift alarm, someone moving a pallet—pulls me off what I’m doing. Then I look back and I’ve entered the wrong inventory numbers. It’s been causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus at home or when you're not at work, too?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work. That’s where it causes trouble—like a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or someone moves a pallet, and I lose the count. Then I end up putting the wrong numbers in inventory. At home… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: I don’t really do a lot of hobbies lately. I mostly notice it at the warehouse—if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet, I lose my place with the count. Then I end up putting in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or getting distracted when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember. School was a long time ago… I mean, I got through it okay, I think. Nothing really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it fidgety. I’ve just been… busier than usual, I guess. Taking on extra safety-improvement stuff at work, and signing up for more art workshops than I normally would. I’ve been making a lot of lists and schedules, like I’m coordinating some big operation.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been easily distracted, or is this a relatively recent problem for you?\n\nPatient: It’s more recent. Maybe the last month or so… since my husband’s been home more and our routine changed. I don’t think I was always like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’ve bought more than I should at craft markets—paints, frames, tools for projects I was sure I’d use. Now the credit card bill is… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than usual. After work I should be tired, but I come home kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting stuff in the garage because just sitting on the couch feels… hard.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with distraction or paying attention when you were a kid in school?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. In school I don’t remember it being a big problem. This feels more like now—at work, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose my place counting and then the inventory numbers come out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and distraction causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. At work I’ve had to go back to my supervisor and double-check things after I messed up inventory counts. At home… my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other, instead of settling into his retirement like we thought we would.\n\nDoctor: How much do you feel your difficulties with focus and distraction are affecting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… it’s affecting things some. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making mistakes on inventory, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of just settling into his retirement.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly cut back on because of your difficulties with focus and distraction?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really stopped doing things, no. It’s more that I have to slow down and double-check at work after I mess up inventory counts. And at home… my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re just walking around each other lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or behaving in school when you were a kid that got you into trouble?\n\nPatient: Not that I really remember. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got distracted sometimes, but I don’t think it was something that got me in trouble at school.\n\nDoctor: How much are your difficulties with focus and distraction interfering with your ability to get along with your husband since he retired?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. It’s more obvious at work. Like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet near me, I lose the count and then I find out I put the wrong numbers in inventory. At home it happens some, but work is where I really notice it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having as much trouble with distractions and focus at home now as you were a month or so ago when this first started?\n\nPatient: A little, but it’s still more of a work thing. That’s where it causes trouble—like a beeper or forklift alarm goes off, or someone moves a pallet, and I lose the count. Then I end up putting the wrong numbers in inventory. At home… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: I don’t really do a lot of hobbies lately. I mostly notice it at the warehouse—if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet, I lose my place with the count. Then I end up putting in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or getting distracted when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember. School was a long time ago… I mean, I got through it okay, I think. Nothing really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it fidgety. I’ve just been… busier than usual, I guess. Taking on extra safety-improvement stuff at work, and signing up for more art workshops than I normally would. I’ve been making a lot of lists and schedules, like I’m coordinating some big operation.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been easily distracted, or is this a relatively recent problem for you?\n\nPatient: It’s more recent. Maybe the last month or so… since my husband’s been home more and our routine changed. I don’t think I was always like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’ve bought more than I should at craft markets—paints, frames, tools for projects I was sure I’d use. Now the credit card bill is… embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more than usual. After work I should be tired, but I come home kind of buzzing. I’ll start reorganizing cabinets or sorting stuff in the garage because just sitting on the couch feels… hard.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with distraction or paying attention when you were a kid in school?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. In school I don’t remember it being a big problem. This feels more like now—at work, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm sounds, I lose my place counting and then the inventory numbers come out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and distraction causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. At work I’ve had to go back to my supervisor and double-check things after I messed up inventory counts. At home… my husband and I have been arguing more. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other, instead of settling into his retirement like we thought we would.\n\nDoctor: How much do you feel your difficulties with focus and distraction are affecting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: I mean… it’s affecting things some. I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor after making mistakes on inventory, which is embarrassing. And at home, my husband and I have been arguing more than I’d like. It feels like we’re kind of walking around each other instead of just settling into his retirement.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to stop doing or significantly cut back on because of your difficulties with focus and distraction?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really stopped doing things, no. It’s more that I have to slow down and double-check at work after I mess up inventory counts. And at home… my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re just walking around each other lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or behaving in school when you were a kid that got you into trouble?\n\nPatient: Not that I really remember. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got distracted sometimes, but I don’t think it was something that got me in trouble at school.\n\nDoctor: How much are your difficulties with focus and distraction interfering with your ability to get along with your husband since he retired?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. It’s not like we’re fighting nonstop, but we’ve been arguing more… and it feels like we’re kind of walking around each other lately instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these distractions and difficulties with focus at home as much as you are at work now?\n\nPatient: Not as much at home, I don’t think. It’s more obvious at work. Like in the warehouse, if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet near me, I lose the count and then I find out I put the wrong numbers in inventory. At home it happens some, but work is where I really notice it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having as much trouble with distractions and focus at home now as you were a month or so ago when this first started?\n\nPatient: A little, but it’s still more of a work thing. At home I get sidetracked some, but the main problem is still at the warehouse—noise or movement pulls me off what I’m counting, and then the numbers come out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how often you get distracted at home over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s changed much at home. It’s still mainly at work that I notice it—like if a beeper goes off or a forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet, I lose the count and then the inventory numbers end up wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the distractions at work, like the beeper or forklift alarm, are the main reason you're having trouble focusing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s like I can’t keep my mind on the count once something goes off around me. The beeper or the forklift alarm pulls me away, and then I end up putting in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
